  - From M: neighbors are E and J.

- Level 3 possibilities toward K:
  - From I to H then to G? I–H is a valid edge; H–G is a valid edge. Then G–K is a valid edge.
    Path: J → I → H → G → K has length 4.
  - From I to B/C then to H/G? I–B is valid; B–C is valid; C–H is valid; H–G is valid; G–K is valid. That gives length 5, longer.

  - From M/E route to F/G/K? M–E is valid; E–F is valid (diagonal). Then F–K is valid.
    Path: J → M → E → F → K has length 4 as well.
  - From I directly to F/G? There is no I–F or I–G edge; verified visually.
  - Any shorter path of length 3?
    - J to something directly adjacent to G or F or X or K: J connects to I, P, M, D. None of these are directly connected to G/F/X/K in one more step except:
      - I to H or B; H connects to G in one more step (making total 4).
      - M to E; E connects to F in one more step (making total 4).
      - P does not connect to F (line crosses without touching), nor to G/X/K.
      - D has no onward connection to G/F/X/K.
    Therefore no path of length 3 exists.

Shortest paths found (length 4):
- J → I → H → G → K
- J → M → E → F → K

Final Answer:
Length: 4, Path: [J, I, H, G, K] or [J, M, E, F, K]

Image name: POGBa_task2_gt3_PAIR4
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gpt-5
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 20
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:

Target nodes: H and O (black).

List all H→O paths by following visible continuous lines:
- Top/upper routes:
  1) H–I–P–O
  2) H–I–C–O
  3) H–B–C–O
- Middle/left routes via G/X/F/E:
  4) H–G–F–E–B–C–O
  5) H–G–F–E–B–C–P–O (via P from C)
  6) H–G–X–F–E–B–C–O (same funnel through F/E)
- Bottom routes:
  7) H–B–N–O
  8) H–B–N–C–O (N–C edge)
- There is no direct H–B–… to O without passing through one of {I, C, P, N} because B connects only to H, E, C, N.

Observation: Every path from H to O must pass through at least one of the nodes {I, C, P, N}. 
- From H to the right, the first branching after H is either via I or via B.
  • If via I, to reach O one must then go through C or P (I connects to H, C, P, J).
  • If via B, the only ways from B toward O are through C or N (B connects to H, E, C, N).
Thus all H→O paths necessarily include one of {C, P, N}. Also if taking via I, it still funnels into {C, P}. Therefore, removing C and P blocks all routes via I and via B (since B→O needs C or N; but if N remains, B→N→O would survive; hence we must also consider N). 

Check minimal cut:
- Single node removal:
  • Remove C: path H–I–P–O remains; also H–B–N–O remains.
  • Remove P: path H–I–C–O or H–B–C–O remains.
  • Remove N: path H–I–P–O or H–I–C–O remains.
So any single node fails.

- Two-node candidates:
  • Remove {C, P}: Paths via I are cut, but H–B–N–O still exists → not sufficient.
  • Remove {C, N}: Cuts B’s alternative through N and any route needing C; but H–I–P–O remains → not sufficient.
  • Remove {P, N}: Cuts I’s route via P and B’s route via N; but H–I–C–O or H–B–C–O remains → not sufficient.

Therefore at least three nodes are required.

- Three-node set {C, P, N}:
  • Any path via I must go to C or P — both removed.
  • Any path via B must go to C or N — both removed.
  • Left detours through G/X/F/E ultimately reach B then need C or N — both removed.
Hence all H→O paths are broken.

No smaller cut works as shown above; thus 3 is minimal.

Final Answer:
Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [C, P, N]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': ['F', 'B', 'I']}

Explore connections from F toward J/P:
- Neighbors of F: E, X, K, G (via slanted edge from F to G).

  - Paths like F–K–J; F–X–K–J; F–E–M–J; F–E–B–…–J (e.g., via H–I–J), F–G–H–I–J, etc.
- Toward P:
  - Paths like F–K–J–P, F–G–H–I–P, F–E–B–C–O–P, F–G–C–O–P, etc.

Key observation: To reach D, the last step is either (J, D) or (P, D). Therefore cutting both of these edges isolates D from the entire graph, regardless of how many routes reach J or P.

Verification:
- Remove (J, D): any path must end via P. Paths from F to P exist (e.g., F–G–H–I–P), so one cut isn’t enough.
- Remove (P, D): any path must end via J. Paths from F to J exist (e.g., F–K–J), so one cut isn’t enough.
- Remove both (J, D) and (P, D): D has no remaining incident edges; thus no path from F (or any node) to D remains.

Minimality:
- At least two edges are necessary because D has degree 2; with only one removed, D remains connected via the other. Therefore size 2 is minimal.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(J, D), (P, D)]

Image name: FD-K_task2_gt2_PAIR2
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gpt-5
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 20
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
- Target nodes are G (top-left) and N (bottom-left). We can remove any other nodes.
- List G’s connections: G is connected to F, K, X, and H (via the diagonal edge to H).
- From G to N, any path must first go from G to one of {F, K, X, H}. Check each branch:

1) Through H: G–H–B–N is a direct 3-edge path. Also from H there are routes H–I–(… )–O–N, and H–E–(… )–O–N, etc. So if H remains, there are multiple paths to N.

2) Through F/K/X: These three form a triangle with each other, and F connects to E; K and X connect only within that triangle and to G. From the K or X branches, to reach N you must go G–(K or X)–F–E–… or G–F–E–… since neither K nor X connects onward except via F. Thus all routes via {F,K,X} funnel through F, then E (because F’s only outward neighbor is E).

- Downstream from E/H side to N: From E you can go E–H–B–N; E–H–I–O–N; E–M–J–P–O–N; E–L–M–…–O–N, etc. From H directly to B–N or via I to O–N. Observations: every path from E toward N must pass through either H or M/L chain? Check E’s neighbors: H, M, L (but L connects only to E and M, so from L you must go to M, then toward J/P/I and then to O–N or to B via H path? M connects to E, L, J. So to reach N from the M side you eventually must use J→P→O→N or J→I→O→N; both rely on O before N or reach B? None connect to B except via H. Therefore, all routes from the E/M side to N eventually require either H→B→N or O→N. So the key chokepoints near N are B and O.

- Near N: N connects to B, C, O, Y. But C connects to B and O and N; Y connects only to N and O. Thus to block access to N without removing N, we must cut both B and O (since there are independent neighbors B and O providing entry; removing only one leaves the other via paths).

- Now combine:
  • If H is removed, the direct G–H–B–N and all H-based routes are gone. Paths via F→E might still reach N through the M/J/P/O side to O–N. Indeed G–F–E–M–J–P–O–N would still work. So removing only H is insufficient.
  • If E is removed, the entire F/K/X branch is cut off from the rest, but G–H–B–N still connects. So E alone is insufficient.

- Minimal cut consideration:
  • Removing H and E together: 
     - G’s connections via H are cut (so no G–H–… paths).
     - G’s other routes via F/K/X must pass F→E, but E is removed, so those routes are blocked.
     - Therefore, no path from G to N remains. This is a cut set of size 2.
  • Can a single node work? 
     - Removing H only: fails (route via F–E–…–O–N).
     - Removing E only: fails (route via H–B–N).
     - Removing F only: G can still go via H; removing K or X only: G can still go via F–E or via H.
     - Removing B only: G–…–O–N remains.
     - Removing O only: G–…–B–N remains.
     - Any other single node on the E→…→O route (like M, J, P, I, L, D, C, Y) can be bypassed via H→B→N; any single node on the H path can be bypassed via F→E→…→O→N. Hence no single-node cut exists.

- Are both nodes in the 2-cut necessary? Yes: 
   • If we keep H but remove E, a path exists via H–B–N. 
   • If we keep E but remove H, a path exists via F–E–M–J–P–O–N. 
  Thus both are required.

Final Answer:
Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [H, E]

  - Path G–F–E–M–J–I still works. So fails.

  - Thus any path from G cannot reach I.

If M removed and H removed, E’s remaining neighbors are L and F. L only connects to M (and E), so with M removed, L is a leaf; dead. F connects to G, K, X; that cluster reconnects only to H via G; with H removed, that route dies. Hence no path from E advances beyond E’s locality. Therefore {H, M} suffices.

Is there a single articulation node? Check H alone: If only H removed, path E–M–J exists. M alone: path E–H–I–J exists. F alone: E–H–… works and E–M–J works. L alone: E–M–J and E–H–… work. Any other single node unlikely to cut both the M route and the H/F route. Therefore minimum is at least 2.

We already removed M; to block the remaining route we must block H (or I or P further right). For instance {M, I} fails due to P, as shown. {M, P} fails due to I. {M, H} works as shown. Also {I, H}? With I and H removed, E–M–J still exists via M, so fails. {P, H}? Fails due to M. {H, D}? Fails via M. Therefore among minimal cuts of size 2, one valid is {H, M}. Another could be {M, G}? With M and G removed, E still has path via H (E–H–I–J). So no. So {H, M} is a clean minimal cut.

  • via H (and the entire left cluster F–G–…): blocked.
  • via L: L only connects to M; with M removed, dead.

Observation: All paths from H to N must leave H via one of its three incident edges: (H,B), (H,E), or (H,I). Cutting all three would isolate H, but we seek a smaller cut.

Check if removing two edges can block all routes:
- If we remove (H,B) and (H,E), paths via H–I remain (e.g., H–I–C–N or H–I–P–O–N).
- If we remove (H,B) and (H,I), paths via H–E remain (e.g., H–E–C–N).
- If we remove (H,E) and (H,I), paths via H–B remain (H–B–N).

Therefore, at least one edge incident to H plus additional downstream cuts might work. Consider keeping only one of H’s exits and block it downstream minimally.

Case 1: Keep only H–B; block B→N region.

But H could also go H–B then to other nodes (e.g., B–C–O–N). Removing (B,C) already covers that. Two edges would suffice if we also prevent H using E or I. So additionally we must remove edges (H,E) and (H,I) or ensure they don’t lead to N independently. They do lead independently, so this plan needs more than two edges unless we cut H’s other exits. Hence not minimal.

Better approach: Cut the three edges out of H: (H,B), (H,E), (H,I). This guarantees disconnection, and any set of two edges fails as shown above.

  - B connects directly to P? No direct edge. B connects to C, I, E. From B we can go to E–P or I–P.
  - O connects by a long edge O–P directly (visible from O to P).
  - Therefore, every N→P path must pass through at least one of {O, I, E}. Specifically:
    • Via O: N–O–P (direct), or N–Y–O–P.
    • Via C/B/I: N–C–I–P, N–B–I–P, or N–B–E–P, N–C–B–E–P, etc.
    • Paths through E require reaching E from B; E also connects to P.
- To disconnect N and P, we must block the direct O–P route by removing O, and also block the C/B/I/E routes.
  - If we remove only O, there remain paths N–C–I–P and N–B–E–P.
  - To block the remaining routes, removing I alone is insufficient because N–B–E–P remains; removing E alone is insufficient because N–C–I–P remains. Hence we must remove at least two among {I, E}.
- Check whether removing {O, I} suffices: Paths via E? N can reach E only through B; from E to P is direct, so N–B–E–P would still exist. So {O, I} is not enough.
- Check {O, E}: Then any path via E is cut. Are there remaining paths? Yes: N–C–I–P (I still present).
- Check {O, I, E}: With O removed and both I and E removed, all N→P routes are severed because:
  • Direct O–P route gone.
  • Any route through C or B must go to P via I or E; both removed.
  • No alternative edges from C or B to P or to J/F/D that lead to P without using I or E (P connects only to O, E, I, and J by visible edges; J connects to P but N has no path to J that avoids I/E/O, since paths to J go through I or E or P itself).
- Verify minimality: Could a 2-node cut work?
  • Any 2-node cut must include O (to stop O–P). The second node could be I or E. If it’s I, path N–B–E–P exists. If it’s E, path N–C–I–P exists. If second is C or B instead, path via the other and I/E remains; and still O–P must be cut (we counted O already). Trying {O, B}: path N–C–I–P remains. {O, C}: path N–B–E–P remains. {O, Y}: O–P still exists unless O removed (we included), but then N–C–I–P remains. Hence no 2-node set suffices.
Therefore, the minimum cut set size is 3: removing O, I, and E.

Final Answer:
Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [O, I, E]

- With P removed: no P-I, and no P-C or P-top.
- With B removed: E cannot reach C via bottom; C might be reachable via top through J->P->C, but P is removed; so C is unreachable from E.
- With F removed: E cannot reach H via the upper-left box; the only other way to H is via B, but B is removed. So H unreachable.

Hence no portal (H, C, P) is reachable from E; thus E disconnected from I.

No 2-node set works as argued above.

- Blocks B-based and J/P-based (since P needs J or O; to reach O needs C which needs B). Remaining route E–F–G–H–I via H exists; also E–M path exists but J removed; M alone doesn’t connect to I. So need also H or F etc. So {B, J} not enough.

Try {H, C}:
- Blocks H route and C route. Remaining: paths via J/P through M, and paths via B not using C (E–B–… to I?). From B to I without H or C: is there B–I edge? No. B connects to E, H, C, N. H removed, C removed, N remains: B–N–O–P–I is a path if those edges exist: N–O exists, O–P exists, P–I exists. Is there a B–N edge? Yes. So E–B–N–O–P–I would still connect. Also E–M–J–I path exists unless we remove M or J. So {H, C} not enough.

Try {H, M}:
- Blocks H route and all J routes.
- Remaining: B/C/N/O/P chain from E via B. Check if E–B–N–O–P–I exists: yes (edges B–N, N–O, O–P, P–I). Also E–B–C–I exists unless C is blocked; but even if C were, the N–O–P path remains. So need B or P additionally.

Try {B, P}:
- Blocks B branch and the P entry.
- Remaining entries: H, C, J.
- H path via F still exists; J path via M still exists. So not enough.

Try {H, B} with an added thought: is there any way E reaches J without M? Edges to J: from M and P and D. P connects to O and I and J. To get to P without B or H, E must go through M or J; that loops. From E to D? E has no path to D except through J/P cluster: D connects only to J and P. So E cannot reach J unless it can reach M or P; reaching M is direct from E. Hence cutting M is crucial to block the J side. Thus any size-2 cut candidate likely needs M plus something that blocks the other side.

Try {M, F}:
- Blocks M/J side (since L funnels to M). Remaining options: through B (to C or to N–O–P) and through H via G.
- But F removal breaks E–F–G–H–I path at the start. Still E–B–… paths remain, so need B or P as well; size 2 not enough.

Try {M, B} we saw leaves only H route; so if we could also rely that H is unavoidable without using B or M—yes, then {M, B} would be size 2 but it isn’t sufficient because H path remains. So minimum cut likely size 3: {B, M, H} or {B, M, F} maybe? Check {B, M, H} validity:
- All M/J routes blocked by M.
- All H routes blocked by H (also blocks F/G funnel).
- B removal blocks reaching C and the N–O–P chain from E. Without B, can E reach P by another way? To reach P you’d need O or J. J requires M (removed). O requires C or N; both require B (removed). So P unreachable.
- Any other path? None.

Can a different size-3 set without B work? Try {M, H, P}:
- Blocks M/J and H, and P entry.
- But E–B–C–I still exists; so need B or C. Thus {M, H, P} insufficient.

Try {M, H, C}:
- Blocks M/J and H and C.
- But E–B–N–O–P–I still exists via P; so need P or B. Hence {M, H, C} insufficient.

Therefore minimal cut size is 3, with one valid cut set {B, M, H}. None of the tested size-2 sets succeeds, and any size-2 seems to leave at least one of the three disjoint “entrances” intact: via H (through F/G), via B→(C or NOP→P), via M→J(→P). You must remove at least one node from two of these plus the third, totaling 3.

- B up to E (vertical) and down-right to C (bent line to C). E connects up to L and right to F and down to the bottom frame to H (via long vertical from E to bottom and then along to H). - G connects up to K and down to H (vertical).
- H connects left to B and down-left via the outer frame to J→M→…→E path, returning to E and F as well.

1) Path N→B→H→G.
2) Path N→B→E→F→K→G (or via E→outer frame→H→G).
3) Path N→C→I→(outer frame)→H→G.
4) Path N→C→O→Y→N loops don’t help; but N→Y→O→P→I→…→H→G also works.

- If we remove H and K, then:
  • Paths via H are gone, so routes ending at H→G are blocked.
  • Remaining route could be N→B→E→F→K→G, but K is removed; thus blocked.
  • Any alternative from F to G without K goes F→X loop back to F only; no other entry to G.
  • Any route avoiding both H and K? None: G’s only neighbors are H and K. So removing {H, K} disconnects N and G.

Is a single-node cut possible?
- Removing H alone leaves path N→B→E→F→K→G.
- Removing K alone leaves path N→B→H→G (or via I→H→G).
- Removing B alone leaves N→C→I→…→H→G and N→C→…→E→F→K→G.
- Removing C alone leaves N→B routes.
- Removing E alone leaves N→B→H→G and N→C→I→…→H→G and N→B→F→K→G (via B→F? No direct; but B→E is needed to reach F; with E removed, still N→B→H→G exists).
- Removing I alone leaves N→B→… paths.
Therefore no single node suffices.

Thus the minimum cut is 2, and {H, K} is valid. Other equivalent 2-cuts include removing both neighbors of G: {H, K} is the unique minimal set that directly isolates G, hence minimal.

4) Verify that removing the set {F, G, K} disconnects X from the rest of the graph, hence from C:
   - After removing F, G, and K, node X has no remaining neighbors (all its incident edges are gone).
   - Therefore no path from X to any node exists, including C.
   - None of the black nodes is removed.

5) Check minimality:
   - Any cut of size 1 fails (X could exit via one of the other two neighbors).
   - Any cut of size 2 fails: 
       • Remove {F, G} → X still connects via K→J→P→O→C.
       • Remove {F, K} → X still connects via G→H→I→O→C or G→E→B→C.
       • Remove {G, K} → X still connects via F→(E/B/J routes)→…→C.
   Hence at least 3 nodes are required, and {F, G, K} works.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [F, G, K]

Minimality:
- Can any single node disconnect H and E? No: removing B leaves H–I–J–M–E and H–G–F–E. Removing M leaves H–B–E and H–G–F–E. Removing F leaves H–B–E and H–I–J–M–E. Any other single node lies on only one of these branches and cannot block all.

  - Remove {B, F}: H–I–J–M–E remains.
  - Remove {M, F}: H–B–E remains.

- Verify no bypass of E:
  - From B, F, O, N, Y, C, H, I, M there is no edge directly to L or any node other than E that connects to L. 
  - E is the unique neighbor of L; edges around F, B, K, X, G do not connect to L except via E.

Therefore, removing E breaks all J→L paths, and no smaller cut than size 1 exists.

- From F directly to the center you can also use E.
